Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I suppose my wife is the main reason I’m sitting here today. She, ah, she practically insisted I come in and talk to someone. 

I mean, I’m a teaching assistant at the university, right? I spend my days grading papers, reading texts, analyzing sentence structures... I know how to look for themes and patterns. That's how my brain works. I pay attention to details. And... well, for the past year, I’ve been noticing these things with her schedule and her communication. 

I know my wife says the late meetings and these... these little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She’ll leave her phone on the counter and a notification pops up, or she'll mention an email, and she claims it's just a new project deadline, or corporate restructuring, or whatever. But if you actually sit down and look at the timeline of it all, they’ve lined up too precisely to feel accidental. 

You don't just have a random late meeting on a Tuesday that perfectly coincides with a sudden shift in the phrasing she uses with her colleagues. It’s... it’s too precise. It’s like there’s a private signal in them. A code, almost, meant to tell me something she won’t say directly to my face. I feel like she's leaving these breadcrumbs for me to figure out, but then when I actually bring up the pattern to her—when I lay out how the dates and the messages connect—she just gets incredibly defensive and tells me I'm overthinking and that I need to see a psychiatrist. So, here I am. But I know what I'm seeing, you know? You don't just have a pattern like that for twelve straight months by sheer coincidence.

Other people? No... no, I haven't noticed anything like that with anyone else. I mean, if you think about it, I interact with dozens, sometimes hundreds of people in a given week. I have my undergraduate students, and let me tell you, most of them can barely construct a coherent thesis statement, let alone orchestrate some sort of multi-layered, covert communication. When they email me at two in the morning asking for an extension on their midterm essay, it’s exactly what it looks like—panic and poor time management. There's no hidden subtext there. 

And my colleagues in the department... well, sure, we spend all day analyzing texts and looking for deeper meanings in literature, but when the department secretary sends out a memo about the copier being out of toner, it's just about the toner. I can tell the difference. I know how to read the baseline of normal, everyday communication. 

And I think that’s exactly why the situation with my wife is so glaring to me. It's the contrast, you see? If it were everyone—if I thought the mailman and the barista and my department head were all sending me signals—well, then maybe I'd question my own... my own perception, I suppose. But it’s the fact that the rest of the world operates on this very literal, straightforward plane, while she has suddenly shifted into this highly synchronized, coded way of operating around me. The juxtaposition is just too sharp to ignore. So, no, it's not anyone else. It's just her. Which is exactly why I know I'm not just pulling this out of thin air, despite what she says.

Well, yes. Yes, absolutely. I mean, like I was trying to explain just a moment ago, I don't come to these conclusions lightly. I am a rational person. I know she claims—and she will tell anyone who asks, I'm sure—that these late meetings and those... those little, seemingly innocuous changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She says it's just the ebb and flow of corporate life, a new quarter, a new initiative, whatever the buzzword of the week is. 

But... well, you have to look at the data. I look at the data. For the past year, I have been observing this, and they have lined up far too precisely to feel accidental. It's not just a gut feeling, doctor, it's a documented observation. It’s like there’s this private signal embedded in the way she operates now, a specific frequency meant only for me, designed to tell me something that she absolutely refuses to say directly to my face. 

And I’ve tried to map it out for her, I really have. I’ve sat down with the calendar, with the timestamps of when she says she’s staying late versus the exact phrasing she uses when she texts me about it, and the correlation is undeniable. The syntax shifts. The punctuation changes. A statistician would look at this and tell you it’s beyond the realm of standard deviation. So, to answer your question, yes, I believe it is definitely true. It has to be. The alternative is that the universe is generating this incredibly complex, twelve-month-long mathematical anomaly just to mess with my head, and frankly, as an academic, I find that much harder to believe than the simple fact that she is communicating through a code. So yes, I am certain of it.

Which is exactly why I know I'm not just pulling this out of thin air, despite what she says.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe it is definitely true that your wife is communicating with you through coded messages?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, absolutely. I mean, like I was trying to explain just a moment ago, I don't come to these conclusions lightly. I am a rational person. I know she claims—and she will tell anyone who asks, I'm sure—that these late meetings and those... those little, seemingly innocuous changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She says it's just the ebb and flow of corporate life, a new quarter, a new initiative, whatever the buzzword of the week is. \n\nBut... well, you have to look at the data. I look at the data. For the past year, I have been observing this, and they have lined up far too precisely to feel accidental. It's not just a gut feeling, doctor, it's a documented observation. It’s like there’s this private signal embedded in the way she operates now, a specific frequency meant only for me, designed to tell me something that she absolutely refuses to say directly to my face. \n\nAnd I’ve tried to map it out for her, I really have. I’ve sat down with the calendar, with the timestamps of when she says she’s staying late versus the exact phrasing she uses when she texts me about it, and the correlation is undeniable. The syntax shifts. The punctuation changes. A statistician would look at this and tell you it’s beyond the realm of standard deviation. So, to answer your question, yes, I believe it is definitely true. It has to be. The alternative is that the universe is generating this incredibly complex, twelve-month-long mathematical anomaly just to mess with my head, and frankly, as an academic, I find that much harder to believe than the simple fact that she is communicating through a code. So yes, I am certain of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, of course it’s possible in the real world. There’s nothing... supernatural or impossible about it. I mean, we’re talking about human behavior, right? We’re talking about language, about scheduling, about the deliberate choices a person makes when they construct a sentence or plan their day. That’s all very grounded in reality. People use codes all the time. Governments use them, corporations use them, hell, even teenagers use them with emojis and slang. It’s a completely natural, documented phenomenon in human communication. 

I’m not sitting here telling you that she’s communicating with me telepathically, or that the messages are being beamed into my head by aliens or... or spirits or something ridiculous like that. I’m not saying she’s manipulating time and space to make her meetings run late. No, no, it’s all very mundane, really, when you strip away the emotional weight of it. It’s just... it’s syntax. It’s timing. It’s the choice to use a passive voice instead of an active one in an email, or the decision to stay at the office until 7:15 PM on a Thursday instead of her usual 6:30 PM. 

These are tangible, measurable things that happen in the physical world. You can track them on a spreadsheet. You can print them out. You can hold the evidence in your hands. So when people disagree with me—when she disagrees with me, or when she tells her friends or her sister that I’m overthinking things—they’re not disagreeing because what I’m suggesting is impossible. They’re disagreeing because they haven’t done the work. They haven’t sat down and analyzed the data the way I have. They just see the surface level of a busy professional woman, and they don’t bother to look at the structural integrity of the communication beneath it. But just because they don’t see it, or don’t want to see it, doesn’t make it supernatural. It just makes it... complex. And maybe a little cowardly on her part, if I’m being honest, to hide behind this elaborate structure instead of just having a straightforward conversation with her husband. But impossible? No. Not at all. It’s entirely, frustratingly real.

But if you actually sit down and look at the timeline of it all, they’ve lined up too precisely to feel accidental. \n\nYou don't just have a random late meeting on a Tuesday that perfectly coincides with a sudden shift in the phrasing she uses with her colleagues. It’s... it’s too precise. It’s like there’s a private signal in them. A code, almost, meant to tell me something she won’t say directly to my face. I feel like she's leaving these breadcrumbs for me to figure out, but then when I actually bring up the pattern to her—when I lay out how the dates and the messages connect—she just gets incredibly defensive and tells me I'm overthinking and that I need to see a psychiatrist. So, here I am. But I know what I'm seeing, you know? You don't just have a pattern like that for twelve straight months by sheer coincidence.\n\nDoctor: Apart from your wife, do you feel that other people are also sending you hidden messages or signals?\n\nPatient: Other people? No... no, I haven't noticed anything like that with anyone else. I mean, if you think about it, I interact with dozens, sometimes hundreds of people in a given week. I have my undergraduate students, and let me tell you, most of them can barely construct a coherent thesis statement, let alone orchestrate some sort of multi-layered, covert communication. When they email me at two in the morning asking for an extension on their midterm essay, it’s exactly what it looks like—panic and poor time management. There's no hidden subtext there. \n\nAnd my colleagues in the department... well, sure, we spend all day analyzing texts and looking for deeper meanings in literature, but when the department secretary sends out a memo about the copier being out of toner, it's just about the toner. I can tell the difference. I know how to read the baseline of normal, everyday communication. \n\nAnd I think that’s exactly why the situation with my wife is so glaring to me. It's the contrast, you see? If it were everyone—if I thought the mailman and the barista and my department head were all sending me signals—well, then maybe I'd question my own... my own perception, I suppose. But it’s the fact that the rest of the world operates on this very literal, straightforward plane, while she has suddenly shifted into this highly synchronized, coded way of operating around me. The juxtaposition is just too sharp to ignore. So, no, it's not anyone else. It's just her. Which is exactly why I know I'm not just pulling this out of thin air, despite what she says.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe it is definitely true that your wife is communicating with you through coded messages?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, absolutely. I mean, like I was trying to explain just a moment ago, I don't come to these conclusions lightly. I am a rational person. I know she claims—and she will tell anyone who asks, I'm sure—that these late meetings and those... those little, seemingly innocuous changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She says it's just the ebb and flow of corporate life, a new quarter, a new initiative, whatever the buzzword of the week is. \n\nBut... well, you have to look at the data. I look at the data. For the past year, I have been observing this, and they have lined up far too precisely to feel accidental. It's not just a gut feeling, doctor, it's a documented observation. It’s like there’s this private signal embedded in the way she operates now, a specific frequency meant only for me, designed to tell me something that she absolutely refuses to say directly to my face. \n\nAnd I’ve tried to map it out for her, I really have. I’ve sat down with the calendar, with the timestamps of when she says she’s staying late versus the exact phrasing she uses when she texts me about it, and the correlation is undeniable. The syntax shifts. The punctuation changes. A statistician would look at this and tell you it’s beyond the realm of standard deviation. So, to answer your question, yes, I believe it is definitely true. It has to be. The alternative is that the universe is generating this incredibly complex, twelve-month-long mathematical anomaly just to mess with my head, and frankly, as an academic, I find that much harder to believe than the simple fact that she is communicating through a code. So yes, I am certain of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your wife’s coded messages are possible in the real world, even if other people disagree, or do you believe they involve something impossible or supernatural?\n\nPatient: Well, no, of course it’s possible in the real world. There’s nothing... supernatural or impossible about it. I mean, we’re talking about human behavior, right? We’re talking about language, about scheduling, about the deliberate choices a person makes when they construct a sentence or plan their day. That’s all very grounded in reality. People use codes all the time. Governments use them, corporations use them, hell, even teenagers use them with emojis and slang. It’s a completely natural, documented phenomenon in human communication. \n\nI’m not sitting here telling you that she’s communicating with me telepathically, or that the messages are being beamed into my head by aliens or... or spirits or something ridiculous like that. I’m not saying she’s manipulating time and space to make her meetings run late. No, no, it’s all very mundane, really, when you strip away the emotional weight of it. It’s just... it’s syntax. It’s timing. It’s the choice to use a passive voice instead of an active one in an email, or the decision to stay at the office until 7:15 PM on a Thursday instead of her usual 6:30 PM. \n\nThese are tangible, measurable things that happen in the physical world. You can track them on a spreadsheet. You can print them out. You can hold the evidence in your hands. So when people disagree with me—when she disagrees with me, or when she tells her friends or her sister that I’m overthinking things—they’re not disagreeing because what I’m suggesting is impossible. They’re disagreeing because they haven’t done the work. They haven’t sat down and analyzed the data the way I have. They just see the surface level of a busy professional woman, and they don’t bother to look at the structural integrity of the communication beneath it. But just because they don’t see it, or don’t want to see it, doesn’t make it supernatural. It just makes it... complex. And maybe a little cowardly on her part, if I’m being honest, to hide behind this elaborate structure instead of just having a straightforward conversation with her husband. But impossible? No. Not at all. It’s entirely, frustratingly real.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent":"Determine whether there have been persistent hallucinations, which would favor schizophrenia or acute psychotic disorder over delusional disorder or paranoid personality disorder.","question":"Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?"}

Voices? No, no, certainly not. I know exactly why you're asking that, of course. You're trying to determine if I've lost touch with reality in a sensory way, if my mind is fabricating things out of thin air. But no, my auditory perception is perfectly intact. 

I spend hours upon hours alone in my office, especially right now during midterms—it's just me, a stack of poorly cited essays, and absolute silence. Well, except for the hum of the HVAC system in the humanities building, which frankly is in desperate need of maintenance, but that's a facilities issue, not a hallucination. If I were hearing voices, I wouldn't be able to do my job. I wouldn't be able to focus on the deep textual analysis required to grade these papers, let alone have the mental clarity to parse out the structural anomalies in my wife's communication. 

So, no, there are no disembodied voices whispering in my ear when no one is around. Everything I am dealing with, everything I've brought to your attention today, is entirely text-based, visual, and behavioral. It's what I read in her emails, what I see in her calendar shifts, the deliberate changes in her syntax. I rely on concrete, observable data, doctor, not phantom sounds. I mean, if I started hearing voices, I'd be the first to march myself over to a neurologist, because that would mean my cognitive faculties were actually failing, rather than just being highly attuned to the patterns my wife is trying to hide from me. So you can cross that off your list. It's just silence when I'm alone.

The syntax shifts. The punctuation changes. A statistician would look at this and tell you it’s beyond the realm of standard deviation. So, to answer your question, yes, I believe it is definitely true. It has to be. The alternative is that the universe is generating this incredibly complex, twelve-month-long mathematical anomaly just to mess with my head, and frankly, as an academic, I find that much harder to believe than the simple fact that she is communicating through a code. So yes, I am certain of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your wife’s coded messages are possible in the real world, even if other people disagree, or do you believe they involve something impossible or supernatural?\n\nPatient: Well, no, of course it’s possible in the real world. There’s nothing... supernatural or impossible about it. I mean, we’re talking about human behavior, right? We’re talking about language, about scheduling, about the deliberate choices a person makes when they construct a sentence or plan their day. That’s all very grounded in reality. People use codes all the time. Governments use them, corporations use them, hell, even teenagers use them with emojis and slang. It’s a completely natural, documented phenomenon in human communication. \n\nI’m not sitting here telling you that she’s communicating with me telepathically, or that the messages are being beamed into my head by aliens or... or spirits or something ridiculous like that. I’m not saying she’s manipulating time and space to make her meetings run late. No, no, it’s all very mundane, really, when you strip away the emotional weight of it. It’s just... it’s syntax. It’s timing. It’s the choice to use a passive voice instead of an active one in an email, or the decision to stay at the office until 7:15 PM on a Thursday instead of her usual 6:30 PM. \n\nThese are tangible, measurable things that happen in the physical world. You can track them on a spreadsheet. You can print them out. You can hold the evidence in your hands. So when people disagree with me—when she disagrees with me, or when she tells her friends or her sister that I’m overthinking things—they’re not disagreeing because what I’m suggesting is impossible. They’re disagreeing because they haven’t done the work. They haven’t sat down and analyzed the data the way I have. They just see the surface level of a busy professional woman, and they don’t bother to look at the structural integrity of the communication beneath it. But just because they don’t see it, or don’t want to see it, doesn’t make it supernatural. It just makes it... complex. And maybe a little cowardly on her part, if I’m being honest, to hide behind this elaborate structure instead of just having a straightforward conversation with her husband. But impossible? No. Not at all. It’s entirely, frustratingly real.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, certainly not. I know exactly why you're asking that, of course. You're trying to determine if I've lost touch with reality in a sensory way, if my mind is fabricating things out of thin air. But no, my auditory perception is perfectly intact. \n\nI spend hours upon hours alone in my office, especially right now during midterms—it's just me, a stack of poorly cited essays, and absolute silence. Well, except for the hum of the HVAC system in the humanities building, which frankly is in desperate need of maintenance, but that's a facilities issue, not a hallucination. If I were hearing voices, I wouldn't be able to do my job. I wouldn't be able to focus on the deep textual analysis required to grade these papers, let alone have the mental clarity to parse out the structural anomalies in my wife's communication. \n\nSo, no, there are no disembodied voices whispering in my ear when no one is around. Everything I am dealing with, everything I've brought to your attention today, is entirely text-based, visual, and behavioral. It's what I read in her emails, what I see in her calendar shifts, the deliberate changes in her syntax. I rely on concrete, observable data, doctor, not phantom sounds. I mean, if I started hearing voices, I'd be the first to march myself over to a neurologist, because that would mean my cognitive faculties were actually failing, rather than just being highly attuned to the patterns my wife is trying to hide from me. So you can cross that off your list. It's just silence when I'm alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Disorganized? Hard to follow? No, I... I don't think anyone has said that. In fact, I'd argue my speech is quite the opposite. As an academic, precision in language is paramount to me. I spend my days instructing undergraduates on how to structure a coherent argument, how to organize their thoughts logically from point A to point B without losing the thread of their thesis. So if anything, I am hyper-organized in my communication. 

I suppose... well, I suppose sometimes when I try to explain the data regarding my wife to people—like my brother, for instance. I tried to walk him through the spreadsheet I made last month, showing the correlation between her use of the passive voice in text messages and the days she claims to have 'budget review' meetings. And he got frustrated, but not because I was disorganized. He just... he couldn't keep up with the variables. He said I was jumping around too much, but I wasn't jumping around, I was simply drawing the necessary connections between the data points. You have to look at the macro and the micro simultaneously to see the pattern, and most people just don't have the patience or the analytical training for that kind of rigorous cross-referencing. 

So, no, no one has told me my behavior or speech is disorganized. They might say I'm intense, or that I'm over-analyzing—which, again, I fundamentally disagree with, because you can't over-analyze a deliberate code—but they don't say I'm hard to follow. If they can't follow me, it's usually a failure on their part to grasp the complexity of the situation, not a failure on my part to articulate it clearly. I lay it all out very methodically. I even use color-coding on the calendar, for heaven's sake, to make it as accessible as possible. So, no, I wouldn't say disorganized at all. Quite the contrary.

And... well, for the past year, I’ve been noticing these things with her schedule and her communication. \n\nI know my wife says the late meetings and these... these little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She’ll leave her phone on the counter and a notification pops up, or she'll mention an email, and she claims it's just a new project deadline, or corporate restructuring, or whatever. But if you actually sit down and look at the timeline of it all, they’ve lined up too precisely to feel accidental. \n\nYou don't just have a random late meeting on a Tuesday that perfectly coincides with a sudden shift in the phrasing she uses with her colleagues. It’s... it’s too precise. It’s like there’s a private signal in them. A code, almost, meant to tell me something she won’t say directly to my face. I feel like she's leaving these breadcrumbs for me to figure out, but then when I actually bring up the pattern to her—when I lay out how the dates and the messages connect—she just gets incredibly defensive and tells me I'm overthinking and that I need to see a psychiatrist. So, here I am. But I know what I'm seeing, you know? You don't just have a pattern like that for twelve straight months by sheer coincidence.\n\nDoctor: Apart from your wife, do you feel that other people are also sending you hidden messages or signals?\n\nPatient: Other people? No... no, I haven't noticed anything like that with anyone else. I mean, if you think about it, I interact with dozens, sometimes hundreds of people in a given week. I have my undergraduate students, and let me tell you, most of them can barely construct a coherent thesis statement, let alone orchestrate some sort of multi-layered, covert communication. When they email me at two in the morning asking for an extension on their midterm essay, it’s exactly what it looks like—panic and poor time management. There's no hidden subtext there. \n\nAnd my colleagues in the department... well, sure, we spend all day analyzing texts and looking for deeper meanings in literature, but when the department secretary sends out a memo about the copier being out of toner, it's just about the toner. I can tell the difference. I know how to read the baseline of normal, everyday communication. \n\nAnd I think that’s exactly why the situation with my wife is so glaring to me. It's the contrast, you see? If it were everyone—if I thought the mailman and the barista and my department head were all sending me signals—well, then maybe I'd question my own... my own perception, I suppose. But it’s the fact that the rest of the world operates on this very literal, straightforward plane, while she has suddenly shifted into this highly synchronized, coded way of operating around me. The juxtaposition is just too sharp to ignore. So, no, it's not anyone else. It's just her. Which is exactly why I know I'm not just pulling this out of thin air, despite what she says.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe it is definitely true that your wife is communicating with you through coded messages?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, absolutely. I mean, like I was trying to explain just a moment ago, I don't come to these conclusions lightly. I am a rational person. I know she claims—and she will tell anyone who asks, I'm sure—that these late meetings and those... those little, seemingly innocuous changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She says it's just the ebb and flow of corporate life, a new quarter, a new initiative, whatever the buzzword of the week is. \n\nBut... well, you have to look at the data. I look at the data. For the past year, I have been observing this, and they have lined up far too precisely to feel accidental. It's not just a gut feeling, doctor, it's a documented observation. It’s like there’s this private signal embedded in the way she operates now, a specific frequency meant only for me, designed to tell me something that she absolutely refuses to say directly to my face. \n\nAnd I’ve tried to map it out for her, I really have. I’ve sat down with the calendar, with the timestamps of when she says she’s staying late versus the exact phrasing she uses when she texts me about it, and the correlation is undeniable. The syntax shifts. The punctuation changes. A statistician would look at this and tell you it’s beyond the realm of standard deviation. So, to answer your question, yes, I believe it is definitely true. It has to be. The alternative is that the universe is generating this incredibly complex, twelve-month-long mathematical anomaly just to mess with my head, and frankly, as an academic, I find that much harder to believe than the simple fact that she is communicating through a code. So yes, I am certain of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your wife’s coded messages are possible in the real world, even if other people disagree, or do you believe they involve something impossible or supernatural?\n\nPatient: Well, no, of course it’s possible in the real world. There’s nothing... supernatural or impossible about it. I mean, we’re talking about human behavior, right? We’re talking about language, about scheduling, about the deliberate choices a person makes when they construct a sentence or plan their day. That’s all very grounded in reality. People use codes all the time. Governments use them, corporations use them, hell, even teenagers use them with emojis and slang. It’s a completely natural, documented phenomenon in human communication. \n\nI’m not sitting here telling you that she’s communicating with me telepathically, or that the messages are being beamed into my head by aliens or... or spirits or something ridiculous like that. I’m not saying she’s manipulating time and space to make her meetings run late. No, no, it’s all very mundane, really, when you strip away the emotional weight of it. It’s just... it’s syntax. It’s timing. It’s the choice to use a passive voice instead of an active one in an email, or the decision to stay at the office until 7:15 PM on a Thursday instead of her usual 6:30 PM. \n\nThese are tangible, measurable things that happen in the physical world. You can track them on a spreadsheet. You can print them out. You can hold the evidence in your hands. So when people disagree with me—when she disagrees with me, or when she tells her friends or her sister that I’m overthinking things—they’re not disagreeing because what I’m suggesting is impossible. They’re disagreeing because they haven’t done the work. They haven’t sat down and analyzed the data the way I have. They just see the surface level of a busy professional woman, and they don’t bother to look at the structural integrity of the communication beneath it. But just because they don’t see it, or don’t want to see it, doesn’t make it supernatural. It just makes it... complex. And maybe a little cowardly on her part, if I’m being honest, to hide behind this elaborate structure instead of just having a straightforward conversation with her husband. But impossible? No. Not at all. It’s entirely, frustratingly real.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, certainly not. If I were hearing voices, I wouldn't be able to do my job. So when people disagree with me—when she disagrees with me, or when she tells her friends or her sister that I’m overthinking things—they’re not disagreeing because what I’m suggesting is impossible. They’re disagreeing because they haven’t done the work. They haven’t sat down and analyzed the data the way I have. They just see the surface level of a busy professional woman, and they don’t bother to look at the structural integrity of the communication beneath it. But just because they don’t see it, or don’t want to see it, doesn’t make it supernatural. It just makes it... complex. And maybe a little cowardly on her part, if I’m being honest, to hide behind this elaborate structure instead of just having a straightforward conversation with her husband. But impossible? No. Not at all. It’s entirely, frustratingly real.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, certainly not. I know exactly why you're asking that, of course. You're trying to determine if I've lost touch with reality in a sensory way, if my mind is fabricating things out of thin air. But no, my auditory perception is perfectly intact. \n\nI spend hours upon hours alone in my office, especially right now during midterms—it's just me, a stack of poorly cited essays, and absolute silence. Well, except for the hum of the HVAC system in the humanities building, which frankly is in desperate need of maintenance, but that's a facilities issue, not a hallucination. If I were hearing voices, I wouldn't be able to do my job. I wouldn't be able to focus on the deep textual analysis required to grade these papers, let alone have the mental clarity to parse out the structural anomalies in my wife's communication. \n\nSo, no, there are no disembodied voices whispering in my ear when no one is around. Everything I am dealing with, everything I've brought to your attention today, is entirely text-based, visual, and behavioral. It's what I read in her emails, what I see in her calendar shifts, the deliberate changes in her syntax. I rely on concrete, observable data, doctor, not phantom sounds. I mean, if I started hearing voices, I'd be the first to march myself over to a neurologist, because that would mean my cognitive faculties were actually failing, rather than just being highly attuned to the patterns my wife is trying to hide from me. So you can cross that off your list. It's just silence when I'm alone.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that your speech or behavior has seemed disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? Hard to follow? No, I... I don't think anyone has said that. In fact, I'd argue my speech is quite the opposite. As an academic, precision in language is paramount to me. I spend my days instructing undergraduates on how to structure a coherent argument, how to organize their thoughts logically from point A to point B without losing the thread of their thesis. So if anything, I am hyper-organized in my communication. \n\nI suppose... well, I suppose sometimes when I try to explain the data regarding my wife to people—like my brother, for instance. I tried to walk him through the spreadsheet I made last month, showing the correlation between her use of the passive voice in text messages and the days she claims to have 'budget review' meetings. And he got frustrated, but not because I was disorganized. He just... he couldn't keep up with the variables. He said I was jumping around too much, but I wasn't jumping around, I was simply drawing the necessary connections between the data points. You have to look at the macro and the micro simultaneously to see the pattern, and most people just don't have the patience or the analytical training for that kind of rigorous cross-referencing. \n\nSo, no, no one has told me my behavior or speech is disorganized. They might say I'm intense, or that I'm over-analyzing—which, again, I fundamentally disagree with, because you can't over-analyze a deliberate code—but they don't say I'm hard to follow. If they can't follow me, it's usually a failure on their part to grasp the complexity of the situation, not a failure on my part to articulate it clearly. I lay it all out very methodically. I even use color-coding on the calendar, for heaven's sake, to make it as accessible as possible. So, no, I wouldn't say disorganized at all. Quite the contrary.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore all of this with your wife started, did you generally find it hard to trust other people?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Trust? Well, no, I wouldn't say I had a generalized issue with trust before all of this. I mean, I'm an academic, so a healthy amount of skepticism is practically a job requirement, right? You don't just take a primary source at face value; you interrogate it, you look for bias, you cross-examine the author's intent. But that's professional rigor, not a personal inability to trust people. 

In my day-to-day life, before my wife started... doing what she's doing... I was perfectly trusting. I trusted my colleagues in the department. I trusted my students, for the most part, unless I caught them plagiarizing, which is a completely different issue. I certainly trusted my wife. We've been married for fifteen years. You don't build a life with someone for a decade and a half if you have a fundamental inability to trust them. I trusted her implicitly. I trusted her when she said she was going to the gym, I trusted her when she said she was working late—back when working late actually meant working late, and not whatever elaborate charade she's orchestrating now. 

I suppose you could say I've always been observant, maybe more observant than the average person. If a colleague said they were fine but their body language suggested otherwise, I'd notice. If a friend canceled dinner plans because they claimed they were sick, but I saw them out getting coffee the next morning, I'd file that away. But I didn't assume malicious intent. I just... I cataloged the inconsistencies. It's just human nature to tell little white lies, to smooth over social interactions. I understood that. I accepted that. 

So, no, I wasn't walking around paranoid or suspicious of everyone I met. I didn't think the barista was out to get me or my neighbors were spying on me. I had a very normal, stable baseline of trust in my relationships. That's what makes this current situation so profoundly disturbing, doctor. It's not that I'm suddenly projecting a lifelong trust issue onto my wife. It's that the person I trusted most in the world has systematically dismantled that trust through these highly specific, deliberate structural changes in her communication. The trust was there. It's the data—the undeniable, empirical data of her behavior—that forced me to re-evaluate it.

I spend my days grading papers, reading texts, analyzing sentence structures... I know how to look for themes and patterns. That's how my brain works. I pay attention to details. And... well, for the past year, I’ve been noticing these things with her schedule and her communication. \n\nI know my wife says the late meetings and these... these little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She’ll leave her phone on the counter and a notification pops up, or she'll mention an email, and she claims it's just a new project deadline, or corporate restructuring, or whatever. But if you actually sit down and look at the timeline of it all, they’ve lined up too precisely to feel accidental. \n\nYou don't just have a random late meeting on a Tuesday that perfectly coincides with a sudden shift in the phrasing she uses with her colleagues. It’s... it’s too precise. It’s like there’s a private signal in them. A code, almost, meant to tell me something she won’t say directly to my face. I feel like she's leaving these breadcrumbs for me to figure out, but then when I actually bring up the pattern to her—when I lay out how the dates and the messages connect—she just gets incredibly defensive and tells me I'm overthinking and that I need to see a psychiatrist. So, here I am. But I know what I'm seeing, you know? You don't just have a pattern like that for twelve straight months by sheer coincidence.\n\nDoctor: Apart from your wife, do you feel that other people are also sending you hidden messages or signals?\n\nPatient: Other people? No... no, I haven't noticed anything like that with anyone else. I mean, if you think about it, I interact with dozens, sometimes hundreds of people in a given week. I have my undergraduate students, and let me tell you, most of them can barely construct a coherent thesis statement, let alone orchestrate some sort of multi-layered, covert communication. When they email me at two in the morning asking for an extension on their midterm essay, it’s exactly what it looks like—panic and poor time management. There's no hidden subtext there. \n\nAnd my colleagues in the department... well, sure, we spend all day analyzing texts and looking for deeper meanings in literature, but when the department secretary sends out a memo about the copier being out of toner, it's just about the toner. I can tell the difference. I know how to read the baseline of normal, everyday communication. \n\nAnd I think that’s exactly why the situation with my wife is so glaring to me. It's the contrast, you see? If it were everyone—if I thought the mailman and the barista and my department head were all sending me signals—well, then maybe I'd question my own... my own perception, I suppose. But it’s the fact that the rest of the world operates on this very literal, straightforward plane, while she has suddenly shifted into this highly synchronized, coded way of operating around me. The juxtaposition is just too sharp to ignore. So, no, it's not anyone else. It's just her. Which is exactly why I know I'm not just pulling this out of thin air, despite what she says.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe it is definitely true that your wife is communicating with you through coded messages?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, absolutely. I mean, like I was trying to explain just a moment ago, I don't come to these conclusions lightly. I am a rational person. I know she claims—and she will tell anyone who asks, I'm sure—that these late meetings and those... those little, seemingly innocuous changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She says it's just the ebb and flow of corporate life, a new quarter, a new initiative, whatever the buzzword of the week is. \n\nBut... well, you have to look at the data. I look at the data. For the past year, I have been observing this, and they have lined up far too precisely to feel accidental. It's not just a gut feeling, doctor, it's a documented observation. It’s like there’s this private signal embedded in the way she operates now, a specific frequency meant only for me, designed to tell me something that she absolutely refuses to say directly to my face. \n\nAnd I’ve tried to map it out for her, I really have. I’ve sat down with the calendar, with the timestamps of when she says she’s staying late versus the exact phrasing she uses when she texts me about it, and the correlation is undeniable. The syntax shifts. The punctuation changes. A statistician would look at this and tell you it’s beyond the realm of standard deviation. So, to answer your question, yes, I believe it is definitely true. It has to be. The alternative is that the universe is generating this incredibly complex, twelve-month-long mathematical anomaly just to mess with my head, and frankly, as an academic, I find that much harder to believe than the simple fact that she is communicating through a code. So yes, I am certain of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your wife’s coded messages are possible in the real world, even if other people disagree, or do you believe they involve something impossible or supernatural?\n\nPatient: Well, no, of course it’s possible in the real world. There’s nothing... supernatural or impossible about it. I mean, we’re talking about human behavior, right? We’re talking about language, about scheduling, about the deliberate choices a person makes when they construct a sentence or plan their day. That’s all very grounded in reality. People use codes all the time. Governments use them, corporations use them, hell, even teenagers use them with emojis and slang. It’s a completely natural, documented phenomenon in human communication. \n\nI’m not sitting here telling you that she’s communicating with me telepathically, or that the messages are being beamed into my head by aliens or... or spirits or something ridiculous like that. I’m not saying she’s manipulating time and space to make her meetings run late. No, no, it’s all very mundane, really, when you strip away the emotional weight of it. It’s just... it’s syntax. It’s timing. It’s the choice to use a passive voice instead of an active one in an email, or the decision to stay at the office until 7:15 PM on a Thursday instead of her usual 6:30 PM. \n\nThese are tangible, measurable things that happen in the physical world. You can track them on a spreadsheet. You can print them out. You can hold the evidence in your hands. So when people disagree with me—when she disagrees with me, or when she tells her friends or her sister that I’m overthinking things—they’re not disagreeing because what I’m suggesting is impossible. They’re disagreeing because they haven’t done the work. They haven’t sat down and analyzed the data the way I have. They just see the surface level of a busy professional woman, and they don’t bother to look at the structural integrity of the communication beneath it. But just because they don’t see it, or don’t want to see it, doesn’t make it supernatural. It just makes it... complex. And maybe a little cowardly on her part, if I’m being honest, to hide behind this elaborate structure instead of just having a straightforward conversation with her husband. But impossible? No. Not at all. It’s entirely, frustratingly real.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, certainly not. I know exactly why you're asking that, of course. You're trying to determine if I've lost touch with reality in a sensory way, if my mind is fabricating things out of thin air. But no, my auditory perception is perfectly intact. \n\nI spend hours upon hours alone in my office, especially right now during midterms—it's just me, a stack of poorly cited essays, and absolute silence. Well, except for the hum of the HVAC system in the humanities building, which frankly is in desperate need of maintenance, but that's a facilities issue, not a hallucination. If I were hearing voices, I wouldn't be able to do my job. I wouldn't be able to focus on the deep textual analysis required to grade these papers, let alone have the mental clarity to parse out the structural anomalies in my wife's communication. \n\nSo, no, there are no disembodied voices whispering in my ear when no one is around. Everything I am dealing with, everything I've brought to your attention today, is entirely text-based, visual, and behavioral. It's what I read in her emails, what I see in her calendar shifts, the deliberate changes in her syntax. I rely on concrete, observable data, doctor, not phantom sounds. I mean, if I started hearing voices, I'd be the first to march myself over to a neurologist, because that would mean my cognitive faculties were actually failing, rather than just being highly attuned to the patterns my wife is trying to hide from me. So you can cross that off your list. It's just silence when I'm alone.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that your speech or behavior has seemed disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? Hard to follow? No, I... I don't think anyone has said that. In fact, I'd argue my speech is quite the opposite. As an academic, precision in language is paramount to me. I spend my days instructing undergraduates on how to structure a coherent argument, how to organize their thoughts logically from point A to point B without losing the thread of their thesis. So if anything, I am hyper-organized in my communication. \n\nI suppose... well, I suppose sometimes when I try to explain the data regarding my wife to people—like my brother, for instance. I tried to walk him through the spreadsheet I made last month, showing the correlation between her use of the passive voice in text messages and the days she claims to have 'budget review' meetings. And he got frustrated, but not because I was disorganized. He just... he couldn't keep up with the variables. He said I was jumping around too much, but I wasn't jumping around, I was simply drawing the necessary connections between the data points. You have to look at the macro and the micro simultaneously to see the pattern, and most people just don't have the patience or the analytical training for that kind of rigorous cross-referencing. \n\nSo, no, no one has told me my behavior or speech is disorganized. They might say I'm intense, or that I'm over-analyzing—which, again, I fundamentally disagree with, because you can't over-analyze a deliberate code—but they don't say I'm hard to follow. If they can't follow me, it's usually a failure on their part to grasp the complexity of the situation, not a failure on my part to articulate it clearly. I lay it all out very methodically. I even use color-coding on the calendar, for heaven's sake, to make it as accessible as possible. So, no, I wouldn't say disorganized at all. Quite the contrary.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this with your wife started, did you generally find it hard to trust other people?\n\nPatient: Trust? Well, no, I wouldn't say I had a generalized issue with trust before all of this. I mean, I'm an academic, so a healthy amount of skepticism is practically a job requirement, right? You don't just take a primary source at face value; you interrogate it, you look for bias, you cross-examine the author's intent. But that's professional rigor, not a personal inability to trust people. \n\nIn my day-to-day life, before my wife started... doing what she's doing... I was perfectly trusting. I trusted my colleagues in the department. I trusted my students, for the most part, unless I caught them plagiarizing, which is a completely different issue. I certainly trusted my wife. We've been married for fifteen years. You don't build a life with someone for a decade and a half if you have a fundamental inability to trust them. I trusted her implicitly. I trusted her when she said she was going to the gym, I trusted her when she said she was working late—back when working late actually meant working late, and not whatever elaborate charade she's orchestrating now. \n\nI suppose you could say I've always been observant, maybe more observant than the average person. If a colleague said they were fine but their body language suggested otherwise, I'd notice. If a friend canceled dinner plans because they claimed they were sick, but I saw them out getting coffee the next morning, I'd file that away. But I didn't assume malicious intent. I just... I cataloged the inconsistencies. It's just human nature to tell little white lies, to smooth over social interactions. I understood that. I accepted that. \n\nSo, no, I wasn't walking around paranoid or suspicious of everyone I met. I didn't think the barista was out to get me or my neighbors were spying on me. I had a very normal, stable baseline of trust in my relationships. That's what makes this current situation so profoundly disturbing, doctor. It's not that I'm suddenly projecting a lifelong trust issue onto my wife. It's that the person I trusted most in the world has systematically dismantled that trust through these highly specific, deliberate structural changes in her communication. The trust was there. It's the data—the undeniable, empirical data of her behavior—that forced me to re-evaluate it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

Euphoric? Unusually high? No, no, absolutely not. I mean, I'm a forty-eight-year-old teaching assistant grading freshman composition papers, doctor. There is very little room for euphoria in my line of work, I assure you. The closest I get to feeling energized is when I find an essay that actually uses a semicolon correctly, and even then, it's more of a quiet, fleeting satisfaction than anything approaching a state of being "high." 

I've never had a period like that, where I'm just buzzing with energy for days on end. I know the kind of thing you're talking about—the manic episodes you see described in abnormal psychology texts, where people stay up for three days straight painting a mural or starting a tech company because they feel invincible. That is entirely foreign to my experience. My energy levels are, and always have been, exceedingly steady. Plodding, even. I wake up at 6:15, I have my coffee, I go to campus, I hold my office hours, I grade, I come home. It's a very metered, predictable existence. 

Even now, with everything going on with my wife, I wouldn't say I'm energized in that euphoric sense. Am I focused? Yes. Am I spending late nights cross-referencing her emails against her credit card statements? Yes, I am. But it's not born out of some grand, sweeping high. It's born out of necessity. It's a meticulous, exhausting process, frankly. I don't feel good doing it. I don't feel on top of the world. I feel like a man who is trying to solve a very complex, very painful puzzle that someone else has deliberately constructed to confuse him. 

So if you're looking for mania, or whatever the clinical term is for running around feeling like a superhero, you won't find it here. I'm just a tired academic trying to make sense of the inconsistencies right in front of me. There's no euphoria in that, doctor. Only a very heavy, very grounded reality.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23.3, F20.0, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose my wife is the main reason I’m sitting here today. She, ah, she practically insisted I come in and talk to someone. \n\nI mean, I’m a teaching assistant at the university, right? I spend my days grading papers, reading texts, analyzing sentence structures... I know how to look for themes and patterns. That's how my brain works. I pay attention to details. And... well, for the past year, I’ve been noticing these things with her schedule and her communication. \n\nI know my wife says the late meetings and these... these little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She’ll leave her phone on the counter and a notification pops up, or she'll mention an email, and she claims it's just a new project deadline, or corporate restructuring, or whatever. But if you actually sit down and look at the timeline of it all, they’ve lined up too precisely to feel accidental. \n\nYou don't just have a random late meeting on a Tuesday that perfectly coincides with a sudden shift in the phrasing she uses with her colleagues. It’s... it’s too precise. It’s like there’s a private signal in them. A code, almost, meant to tell me something she won’t say directly to my face. I feel like she's leaving these breadcrumbs for me to figure out, but then when I actually bring up the pattern to her—when I lay out how the dates and the messages connect—she just gets incredibly defensive and tells me I'm overthinking and that I need to see a psychiatrist. So, here I am. But I know what I'm seeing, you know? You don't just have a pattern like that for twelve straight months by sheer coincidence.\n\nDoctor: Apart from your wife, do you feel that other people are also sending you hidden messages or signals?\n\nPatient: Other people? No... no, I haven't noticed anything like that with anyone else. I mean, if you think about it, I interact with dozens, sometimes hundreds of people in a given week. I have my undergraduate students, and let me tell you, most of them can barely construct a coherent thesis statement, let alone orchestrate some sort of multi-layered, covert communication. When they email me at two in the morning asking for an extension on their midterm essay, it’s exactly what it looks like—panic and poor time management. There's no hidden subtext there. \n\nAnd my colleagues in the department... well, sure, we spend all day analyzing texts and looking for deeper meanings in literature, but when the department secretary sends out a memo about the copier being out of toner, it's just about the toner. I can tell the difference. I know how to read the baseline of normal, everyday communication. \n\nAnd I think that’s exactly why the situation with my wife is so glaring to me. It's the contrast, you see? If it were everyone—if I thought the mailman and the barista and my department head were all sending me signals—well, then maybe I'd question my own... my own perception, I suppose. But it’s the fact that the rest of the world operates on this very literal, straightforward plane, while she has suddenly shifted into this highly synchronized, coded way of operating around me. The juxtaposition is just too sharp to ignore. So, no, it's not anyone else. It's just her. Which is exactly why I know I'm not just pulling this out of thin air, despite what she says.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe it is definitely true that your wife is communicating with you through coded messages?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, absolutely. I mean, like I was trying to explain just a moment ago, I don't come to these conclusions lightly. I am a rational person. I know she claims—and she will tell anyone who asks, I'm sure—that these late meetings and those... those little, seemingly innocuous changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She says it's just the ebb and flow of corporate life, a new quarter, a new initiative, whatever the buzzword of the week is. \n\nBut... well, you have to look at the data. I look at the data. For the past year, I have been observing this, and they have lined up far too precisely to feel accidental. It's not just a gut feeling, doctor, it's a documented observation. It’s like there’s this private signal embedded in the way she operates now, a specific frequency meant only for me, designed to tell me something that she absolutely refuses to say directly to my face. \n\nAnd I’ve tried to map it out for her, I really have. I’ve sat down with the calendar, with the timestamps of when she says she’s staying late versus the exact phrasing she uses when she texts me about it, and the correlation is undeniable. The syntax shifts. The punctuation changes. A statistician would look at this and tell you it’s beyond the realm of standard deviation. So, to answer your question, yes, I believe it is definitely true. It has to be. The alternative is that the universe is generating this incredibly complex, twelve-month-long mathematical anomaly just to mess with my head, and frankly, as an academic, I find that much harder to believe than the simple fact that she is communicating through a code. So yes, I am certain of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your wife’s coded messages are possible in the real world, even if other people disagree, or do you believe they involve something impossible or supernatural?\n\nPatient: Well, no, of course it’s possible in the real world. There’s nothing... supernatural or impossible about it. I mean, we’re talking about human behavior, right? We’re talking about language, about scheduling, about the deliberate choices a person makes when they construct a sentence or plan their day. That’s all very grounded in reality. People use codes all the time. Governments use them, corporations use them, hell, even teenagers use them with emojis and slang. It’s a completely natural, documented phenomenon in human communication. \n\nI’m not sitting here telling you that she’s communicating with me telepathically, or that the messages are being beamed into my head by aliens or... or spirits or something ridiculous like that. I’m not saying she’s manipulating time and space to make her meetings run late. No, no, it’s all very mundane, really, when you strip away the emotional weight of it. It’s just... it’s syntax. It’s timing. It’s the choice to use a passive voice instead of an active one in an email, or the decision to stay at the office until 7:15 PM on a Thursday instead of her usual 6:30 PM. \n\nThese are tangible, measurable things that happen in the physical world. You can track them on a spreadsheet. You can print them out. You can hold the evidence in your hands. So when people disagree with me—when she disagrees with me, or when she tells her friends or her sister that I’m overthinking things—they’re not disagreeing because what I’m suggesting is impossible. They’re disagreeing because they haven’t done the work. They haven’t sat down and analyzed the data the way I have. They just see the surface level of a busy professional woman, and they don’t bother to look at the structural integrity of the communication beneath it. But just because they don’t see it, or don’t want to see it, doesn’t make it supernatural. It just makes it... complex. And maybe a little cowardly on her part, if I’m being honest, to hide behind this elaborate structure instead of just having a straightforward conversation with her husband. But impossible? No. Not at all. It’s entirely, frustratingly real.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, certainly not. I know exactly why you're asking that, of course. You're trying to determine if I've lost touch with reality in a sensory way, if my mind is fabricating things out of thin air. But no, my auditory perception is perfectly intact. \n\nI spend hours upon hours alone in my office, especially right now during midterms—it's just me, a stack of poorly cited essays, and absolute silence. Well, except for the hum of the HVAC system in the humanities building, which frankly is in desperate need of maintenance, but that's a facilities issue, not a hallucination. If I were hearing voices, I wouldn't be able to do my job. I wouldn't be able to focus on the deep textual analysis required to grade these papers, let alone have the mental clarity to parse out the structural anomalies in my wife's communication. \n\nSo, no, there are no disembodied voices whispering in my ear when no one is around. Everything I am dealing with, everything I've brought to your attention today, is entirely text-based, visual, and behavioral. It's what I read in her emails, what I see in her calendar shifts, the deliberate changes in her syntax. I rely on concrete, observable data, doctor, not phantom sounds. I mean, if I started hearing voices, I'd be the first to march myself over to a neurologist, because that would mean my cognitive faculties were actually failing, rather than just being highly attuned to the patterns my wife is trying to hide from me. So you can cross that off your list. It's just silence when I'm alone.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that your speech or behavior has seemed disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? Hard to follow? No, I... I don't think anyone has said that. In fact, I'd argue my speech is quite the opposite. As an academic, precision in language is paramount to me. I spend my days instructing undergraduates on how to structure a coherent argument, how to organize their thoughts logically from point A to point B without losing the thread of their thesis. So if anything, I am hyper-organized in my communication. \n\nI suppose... well, I suppose sometimes when I try to explain the data regarding my wife to people—like my brother, for instance. I tried to walk him through the spreadsheet I made last month, showing the correlation between her use of the passive voice in text messages and the days she claims to have 'budget review' meetings. And he got frustrated, but not because I was disorganized. He just... he couldn't keep up with the variables. He said I was jumping around too much, but I wasn't jumping around, I was simply drawing the necessary connections between the data points. You have to look at the macro and the micro simultaneously to see the pattern, and most people just don't have the patience or the analytical training for that kind of rigorous cross-referencing. \n\nSo, no, no one has told me my behavior or speech is disorganized. They might say I'm intense, or that I'm over-analyzing—which, again, I fundamentally disagree with, because you can't over-analyze a deliberate code—but they don't say I'm hard to follow. If they can't follow me, it's usually a failure on their part to grasp the complexity of the situation, not a failure on my part to articulate it clearly. I lay it all out very methodically. I even use color-coding on the calendar, for heaven's sake, to make it as accessible as possible. So, no, I wouldn't say disorganized at all. Quite the contrary.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this with your wife started, did you generally find it hard to trust other people?\n\nPatient: Trust? Well, no, I wouldn't say I had a generalized issue with trust before all of this. I mean, I'm an academic, so a healthy amount of skepticism is practically a job requirement, right? You don't just take a primary source at face value; you interrogate it, you look for bias, you cross-examine the author's intent. But that's professional rigor, not a personal inability to trust people. \n\nIn my day-to-day life, before my wife started... doing what she's doing... I was perfectly trusting. I trusted my colleagues in the department. I trusted my students, for the most part, unless I caught them plagiarizing, which is a completely different issue. I certainly trusted my wife. We've been married for fifteen years. You don't build a life with someone for a decade and a half if you have a fundamental inability to trust them. I trusted her implicitly. I trusted her when she said she was going to the gym, I trusted her when she said she was working late—back when working late actually meant working late, and not whatever elaborate charade she's orchestrating now. \n\nI suppose you could say I've always been observant, maybe more observant than the average person. If a colleague said they were fine but their body language suggested otherwise, I'd notice. If a friend canceled dinner plans because they claimed they were sick, but I saw them out getting coffee the next morning, I'd file that away. But I didn't assume malicious intent. I just... I cataloged the inconsistencies. It's just human nature to tell little white lies, to smooth over social interactions. I understood that. I accepted that. \n\nSo, no, I wasn't walking around paranoid or suspicious of everyone I met. I didn't think the barista was out to get me or my neighbors were spying on me. I had a very normal, stable baseline of trust in my relationships. That's what makes this current situation so profoundly disturbing, doctor. It's not that I'm suddenly projecting a lifelong trust issue onto my wife. It's that the person I trusted most in the world has systematically dismantled that trust through these highly specific, deliberate structural changes in her communication. The trust was there. It's the data—the undeniable, empirical data of her behavior—that forced me to re-evaluate it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or euphoric for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Euphoric? Unusually high? No, no, absolutely not. I mean, I'm a forty-eight-year-old teaching assistant grading freshman composition papers, doctor. There is very little room for euphoria in my line of work, I assure you. The closest I get to feeling energized is when I find an essay that actually uses a semicolon correctly, and even then, it's more of a quiet, fleeting satisfaction than anything approaching a state of being \"high.\" \n\nI've never had a period like that, where I'm just buzzing with energy for days on end. I know the kind of thing you're talking about—the manic episodes you see described in abnormal psychology texts, where people stay up for three days straight painting a mural or starting a tech company because they feel invincible. That is entirely foreign to my experience. My energy levels are, and always have been, exceedingly steady. Plodding, even. I wake up at 6:15, I have my coffee, I go to campus, I hold my office hours, I grade, I come home. It's a very metered, predictable existence. \n\nEven now, with everything going on with my wife, I wouldn't say I'm energized in that euphoric sense. Am I focused? Yes. Am I spending late nights cross-referencing her emails against her credit card statements? Yes, I am. But it's not born out of some grand, sweeping high. It's born out of necessity. It's a meticulous, exhausting process, frankly. I don't feel good doing it. I don't feel on top of the world. I feel like a man who is trying to solve a very complex, very painful puzzle that someone else has deliberately constructed to confuse him. \n\nSo if you're looking for mania, or whatever the clinical term is for running around feeling like a superhero, you won't find it here. I'm just a tired academic trying to make sense of the inconsistencies right in front of me. There's no euphoria in that, doctor. Only a very heavy, very grounded reality.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

The closest I get to feeling energized is when I find an essay that actually uses a semicolon correctly, and even then, it's more of a quiet, fleeting satisfaction than anything approaching a state of being \"high.\" \n\nI've never had a period like that, where I'm just buzzing with energy for days on end. That is entirely foreign to my experience. My energy levels are, and always have been, exceedingly steady. Plodding, even. I wake up at 6:15, I have my coffee, I go to campus, I hold my office hours, I grade, I come home. It's a very metered, predictable existence. \n\nEven now, with everything going on with my wife, I wouldn't say I'm energized in that euphoric sense. Am I focused? Yes. Am I spending late nights cross-referencing her emails against her credit card statements? Yes, I am. But it's not born out of some grand, sweeping high. It's born out of necessity. It's a meticulous, exhausting process, frankly. I don't feel good doing it. I don't feel on top of the world. I feel like a man who is trying to solve a very complex, very painful puzzle that someone else has deliberately constructed to confuse him. \n\nSo if you're looking for mania, or whatever the clinical term is for running around feeling like a superhero, you won't find it here. Work? No, no, absolutely not. I mean, as I mentioned, my schedule is highly structured. If anything, the discipline required to maintain my teaching duties has been a... a grounding force, really. She completely refuses to engage with the empirical evidence, which leads to these protracted, exhausting arguments in the living room that go round and round. 

It actually reached a point where we started seeing a couples therapist a little while ago. I thought having a neutral, objective third party in the room would help mediate, perhaps finally get my wife to acknowledge the inconsistencies I’ve been documenting. But instead of focusing on the structural anomalies in her behavior, the therapist... well, she took a rather unexpected tack. After a few sessions of me simply explaining my methodology and showing her the data, the couples therapist essentially stopped focusing on our dynamic and suggested that *I* get an individual assessment. 

Which is, frankly, why I am sitting here talking to you right now. I agreed to come just to demonstrate, definitively, that my faculties are perfectly intact and that my work and routines are entirely undisturbed. The issue is the tension at home, doctor, not with my ability to function in the world.
